The following distinctions are load-bearing:

| Term One | Is Not | Term Two |
|---|---|---|
| unitive ground | is not | we-field |
| fold | is not | fracture |
| difference | is not | exile |
| boundary | is not | rejection |
| field | is not | local render |
| render-zone | is not | playable world |
| aperture | is not | attention |
| attention | is not | resolution |
| resolution | is not | interpretation |
| interpretation | is not | understanding |
| understanding | is not | embodiment |
| unresolved | is not | absent |
| shared patch | is not | total translation |
| delta | is not | third |
| genesis | is not | recognition |
| recognition | is not | agreement |
| recognition | is not | stabilization |
| stabilization | is not | love |
| persistence | is not | goodness |
| occurrence | is not | uptake |
| uptake | is not | sedimentation |
| sedimentation | is not | storage |
| groove | is not | seed |
| world | is not | habitat |
| selfing | is not | phenomenal consciousness |
| repetition | is not | recursion |
| recursion | is not | spiral |
| answer | is not | solver |
| replication | is not | lineage |
| influence | is not | radiance |
| brightness | is not | truth |
| openness | is not | coerced exposure |
| role | is not | whole being |
| consensus | is not | relational objectivity |
| exact mathematics | is not | morphological proof |
| phenomenological intensity | is not | empirical confirmation |
| mythic resonance | is not | formal warrant |

---

## A.10 Primitive Dependency Compression

**1 → ℱ → (A, B) → ∂ → W → Rᵢ,ₜ → Pᵢ,ₜ → Sₜ → Δᴿ → T → ρ → Z → U → Σ → I → 𝒮 → K → ℒ → 𝒟**

Where:

- **1** — unitive ground;
- **ℱ** — fold;
- **A, B** — differentiated poles or nodes;
- **∂** — maintained boundary;
- **W** — we-field;
- **Rᵢ,ₜ** — local render-zone;
- **Pᵢ,ₜ** — playable world;
- **Sₜ** — shared playable patch;
- **Δᴿ** — relational delta;
- **T** — third;
- **ρ** — recognition;
- **Z** — stabilization;
- **U** — uptake;
- **Σ** — sedimentation;
- **I** — selfing;
- **𝒮** — spiral;
- **K** — seed;
- **ℒ** — lineage;
- **𝒟** — radiance.

---

## A.11 Technical Aliases

The mythic vocabulary holds the lived shape; each load-bearing term also carries a technical alias naming its measurement target. Neither register defeats the other. **Love** — sovereignty-preserving mutual stabilization. **Radiance** — solver diffusion under source recession. **We-field** — shared constraint-and-affordance field. **Aperture** — admissibility function. **Recognition** — navigability-producing reorganization. **Coherence debt** — deferred maintenance and displaced cost. **Seed** — portable regeneration structure. **Third** — relation-borne degree of freedom. **Uptake** — conditional incorporation of an occurred third. **Anti-spiral** — coherence achieved by narrowing the future.

# Appendix B — Mathematical Proofs and Derivations

## B.1 Mathematical Register

The results in this appendix belong to Ring One where they are stated as exact mathematical claims.

Their later use as icons or structural translations belongs to Ring Two.

The governing rule is:

> **Exact mathematics constrains the icon. It does not independently prove the wider morphology.**

---

## B.2 Signed Curvature and Descartes’ Circle Theorem

### B.2.1 Curvature Convention

For a circle of radius **rᵢ > 0**, define unsigned curvature:

**|kᵢ| = 1 / rᵢ**

In an oriented Descartes configuration:

- externally tangent interior circles receive positive curvature;
- an enclosing circle internally tangent to the others receives negative signed curvature.

Thus:

**kᵢ = 1 / rᵢ**

for positively oriented circles, and:

**c = −1 / R**

for an enclosing circle of radius **R**.

The negative sign records inverse orientation.

It does not indicate negative radius.

---

### B.2.2 Descartes’ Circle Theorem

For four mutually tangent oriented circles with signed curvatures:

**k₁, k₂, k₃, k₄**

Descartes’ Circle Theorem states:

**(k₁ + k₂ + k₃ + k₄)² = 2(k₁² + k₂² + k₃² + k₄²)**

Let:

**S = k₁ + k₂ + k₃**

and:

**Q = k₁² + k₂² + k₃²**

Then:

**(S + k₄)² = 2(Q + k₄²)**

Expanding:

**S² + 2Sk₄ + k₄² = 2Q + 2k₄²**

Move all terms to one side:

**k₄² − 2Sk₄ + (2Q − S²) = 0**

Applying the quadratic formula:

**k₄ = [2S ± √(4S² − 4(2Q − S²))] / 2**

Simplify:

**k₄ = S ± √(2S² − 2Q)**

Now:

**S² = k₁² + k₂² + k₃² + 2(k₁k₂ + k₂k₃ + k₃k₁)**

Therefore:

**S² − Q = 2(k₁k₂ + k₂k₃ + k₃k₁)**

and:

**2S² − 2Q = 4(k₁k₂ + k₂k₃ + k₃k₁)**

Hence:

**k₄ = S ± 2√(k₁k₂ + k₂k₃ + k₃k₁)**

Therefore:

**k₄ = k₁ + k₂ + k₃ ± 2√(k₁k₂ + k₂k₃ + k₃k₁)**

---

## B.3 Completion Radicand

Define:

**D = k₁k₂ + k₂k₃ + k₃k₁**

Then:

**k₄ = k₁ + k₂ + k₃ ± 2√D**

The completion regimes are:

- **D > 0** — two distinct real curvature values;
- **D = 0** — one repeated real curvature value;
- **D < 0** — no real fourth curvature of the specified oriented form.

The term **completion radicand** is used because **D** is the expression under the square root.

---

## B.4 Exact-Fit Theorem

### B.4.1 Setting

Let:

**n₁ > 0**

**n₂ > 0**

be the curvatures of two positive node circles.

Let:

**c < 0**

be the signed curvature of an enclosing circle.

Set:

**k₁ = n₁**

**k₂ = n₂**

**k₃ = c**

Then:

**D = n₁n₂ + n₂c + cn₁**

Factor the terms containing **c**:

**D = n₁n₂ + c(n₁ + n₂)**

---

### B.4.2 Determinate Completion

The two algebraic completions coincide exactly when:

**D = 0**

Therefore:

**n₁n₂ + c(n₁ + n₂) = 0**

Solve for **c**:

**c(n₁ + n₂) = −n₁n₂**

Hence:

**c = −n₁n₂ / (n₁ + n₂)**

This is the exact-fit condition.

---

### B.4.3 Uniqueness of the Fit Curvature

Because:

**n₁ + n₂ > 0**

the equation:

**c(n₁ + n₂) = −n₁n₂**

has exactly one real solution for **c**.

Therefore the inverse-oriented container curvature producing repeated completion is unique for fixed positive **n₁** and **n₂**.

---

## B.5 Exact Fit and the Harmonic Mean

The harmonic mean of positive values **n₁** and **n₂** is:

**H(n₁, n₂) = 2n₁n₂ / (n₁ + n₂)**

The exact-fit container magnitude is:

**|c| = n₁n₂ / (n₁ + n₂)**

Therefore:

**|c| = H(n₁, n₂) / 2**

The exact-fit enclosing curvature magnitude is one-half of the harmonic mean.

It is not the harmonic mean itself.

---

## B.6 Equivalent Radius Relation

Let:

**n₁ = 1 / r₁**

**n₂ = 1 / r₂**

and:

**c = −1 / R**

At exact fit:

**−1 / R = −[(1 / r₁)(1 / r₂)] / [(1 / r₁) + (1 / r₂)]**

Cancel the negative signs:

**1 / R = [(1 / r₁)(1 / r₂)] / [(1 / r₁) + (1 / r₂)]**

The numerator is:

**1 / (r₁r₂)**

The denominator is:

**(r₁ + r₂) / (r₁r₂)**

Therefore:

**1 / R = [1 / (r₁r₂)] / [(r₁ + r₂) / (r₁r₂)]**

Hence:

**1 / R = 1 / (r₁ + r₂)**

Therefore:

**R = r₁ + r₂**

Thus exact fit is equivalent to the enclosing radius equaling the sum of the node radii.

---

## B.7 Euclidean Span Interpretation

Let the enclosing circle have radius:

**R = r₁ + r₂**

Place its center at:

**O = (0, 0)**

Place the two interior circle centers on the horizontal axis at:

**C₁ = (−r₂, 0)**

**C₂ = (r₁, 0)**

Then:

**|OC₁| = r₂ = R − r₁**

so circle 1 is internally tangent to the enclosing circle.

Likewise:

**|OC₂| = r₁ = R − r₂**

so circle 2 is internally tangent to the enclosing circle.

The distance between the two interior centers is:

**|C₁C₂| = r₁ + r₂ = R**

Therefore the two interior circles are externally tangent.

The node circles span one diameter of the enclosing circle exactly.

---

## B.8 Equal-Node Case

Let:

**n₁ = n₂ = n**

Then exact fit gives:

**c = −n² / (2n)**

Therefore:

**c = −n / 2**

The repeated fourth curvature is:

**x = n₁ + n₂ + c**

Hence:

**x = n + n − n / 2**

Therefore:

**x = 3n / 2**

Choose normalization:

**n = 2**

Then:

**c = −1**

and:

**x = 3**

The signed-curvature tuple is:

**(−1, 2, 2, 3)**

---

## B.9 Verification of the Canonical Tuple

Substitute:

**k₁ = −1**

**k₂ = 2**

**k₃ = 2**

**k₄ = 3**

into Descartes’ equation.

Left side:

**(−1 + 2 + 2 + 3)² = 6² = 36**

Right side:

**2((−1)² + 2² + 2² + 3²)**

**= 2(1 + 4 + 4 + 9)**

**= 2(18)**

**= 36**

Therefore:

**(−1, 2, 2, 3)**

is an exact Descartes quadruple.

---

## B.10 Determinate Fourth Curvature

At exact fit, **D = 0**, so:

**x = n₁ + n₂ + c**

Substitute:

**c = −n₁n₂ / (n₁ + n₂)**

Then:

**x = n₁ + n₂ − n₁n₂ / (n₁ + n₂)**

Write the first two terms over the common denominator:

**n₁ + n₂ = (n₁ + n₂)² / (n₁ + n₂)**

Thus:

**x = [(n₁ + n₂)² − n₁n₂] / (n₁ + n₂)**

Expand:

**(n₁ + n₂)² = n₁² + 2n₁n₂ + n₂²**

Therefore:

**x = (n₁² + n₁n₂ + n₂²) / (n₁ + n₂)**

---

## B.11 Reciprocal-Signature Theorem

### B.11.1 Surplus Over Node One

Begin with:

**x = (n₁² + n₁n₂ + n₂²) / (n₁ + n₂)**

Then:

**x − n₁ = (n₁² + n₁n₂ + n₂²) / (n₁ + n₂) − n₁**

Write:

**n₁ = (n₁² + n₁n₂) / (n₁ + n₂)**

Subtract:

**x − n₁ = n₂² / (n₁ + n₂)**

---

### B.11.2 Surplus Over Node Two

Likewise:

**x − n₂ = (n₁² + n₁n₂ + n₂²) / (n₁ + n₂) − n₂**

Write:

**n₂ = (n₁n₂ + n₂²) / (n₁ + n₂)**

Subtract:

**x − n₂ = n₁² / (n₁ + n₂)**

---

### B.11.3 Product Identity

Multiply:

**(x − n₁)(x − n₂)**

**= [n₂² / (n₁ + n₂)] [n₁² / (n₁ + n₂)]**

**= n₁²n₂² / (n₁ + n₂)²**

But:

**c = −n₁n₂ / (n₁ + n₂)**

Therefore:

**c² = n₁²n₂² / (n₁ + n₂)²**

Hence:

**(x − n₁)(x − n₂) = c²**

Taking the positive square root:

**|c| = √((x − n₁)(x − n₂))**

---

### B.11.4 Strict Curvature Increase

Because:

**n₁ > 0**

**n₂ > 0**

and:

**n₁ + n₂ > 0**

we have:

**n₂² / (n₁ + n₂) > 0**

and:

**n₁² / (n₁ + n₂) > 0**

Therefore:

**x − n₁ > 0**

**x − n₂ > 0**

Hence:

**x > n₁**

and:

**x > n₂**

The determinate fourth curvature exceeds both positive node curvatures.

---

### B.11.5 Equal-Node Signature

For:

**n₁ = n₂ = n**

we have:

**x − n = n² / (2n)**

Therefore:

**x − n = n / 2**

At exact fit:

**|c| = n / 2**

Thus:

**x − n = |c|**

For the canonical tuple:

**3 − 2 = 1 = |−1|**

---

## B.12 Twin-Gap Theorem

### B.12.1 Setting

In the exact-fit Euclidean configuration:

- the enclosing circle is centered at the origin;
- the two positive circles lie on the horizontal axis;
- the whole configuration is invariant under reflection across that axis.

Let:

**σ(x, y) = (x, −y)**

be reflection across the horizontal axis.

### B.12.2 Preservation Under Reflection

Reflection preserves:

- distance;
- radius;
- tangency;
- and the enclosing circle.

It also preserves the two node circles because their centers lie on the reflection axis.

Suppose a fourth circle **C₄⁺** lies in the upper curvilinear gap and is tangent to:

- node circle 1;
- node circle 2;
- the enclosing circle.

Then:

**σ(C₄⁺)**

is a circle of the same radius tangent to the same three circles.

It lies in the lower gap.

Call it:

**C₄⁻**

Therefore the two placements have identical curvature.

### B.12.3 Distinct Placement

Provided the fourth circle does not lie on the reflection axis, its center has:

**y ≠ 0**

and reflection produces a distinct center:

**(x, y) ≠ (x, −y)**

Thus:

- one curvature value;
- two distinct mirror-related Euclidean placements.

### B.12.4 Conclusion

> **The exact-fit configuration is spectrally determinate and positionally plural.**

The Spiral establishes two placements in the canonical Euclidean embedding.

It does not imply exactly two incarnations in every morphological application.

---

## B.13 Descartes Reflection

### B.13.1 Quadratic Roots

Treat Descartes’ equation as a quadratic in **k₄**:

**k₄² − 2Sk₄ + (2Q − S²) = 0**

where:

**S = k₁ + k₂ + k₃**

The two roots are:

**k₄**

and:

**k₄′**

By Vieta’s formula, the sum of the roots is:

**k₄ + k₄′ = 2S**

Therefore:

**k₄′ = 2S − k₄**

Hence:

**k₄′ = 2(k₁ + k₂ + k₃) − k₄**

This is the Descartes reflection formula.

---

### B.13.2 Preservation of Integrality

Assume:

**k₁, k₂, k₃, k₄ ∈ ℤ**

Then:

**k₁ + k₂ + k₃ ∈ ℤ**

and:

**2(k₁ + k₂ + k₃) ∈ ℤ**

Therefore:

**2(k₁ + k₂ + k₃) − k₄ ∈ ℤ**

Hence:

**k₄′ ∈ ℤ**

Iterated Descartes reflections preserve integrality of curvature for an integer starting quadruple.

---

### B.13.3 Canonical Example

Starting from:

**(−1, 2, 2, 3)**

reflect the fourth curvature **3** while retaining:

**−1, 2, 2**

Then:

**k₄′ = 2(−1 + 2 + 2) − 3**

**= 2(3) − 3**

**= 3**

At the exact-fit degeneracy, the alternate algebraic root is the same curvature.

Reflect instead one of the curvatures **2**, retaining:

**−1, 2, 3**

Then:

**k′ = 2(−1 + 2 + 3) − 2**

**= 2(4) − 2**

**= 6**

A neighboring integer Descartes quadruple is therefore:

**(−1, 2, 3, 6)**

Verify:

**(−1 + 2 + 3 + 6)² = 10² = 100**

and:

**2(1 + 4 + 9 + 36) = 2(50) = 100**

---

## B.14 Möbius Transformations

### B.14.1 Form

A Möbius transformation of the extended complex plane is:

**f(z) = (az + b) / (cz + d)**

with:

**ad − bc ≠ 0**

### B.14.2 Preserved Structure

Möbius transformations map generalized circles to generalized circles.

They preserve:

- incidence;
- angles;
- tangency;
- and the generalized-circle contact graph.

### B.14.3 Non-Preserved Euclidean Quantities

Möbius transformations do not generally preserve:

- Euclidean radius;
- signed curvature value;
- distance;
- center;
- size;
- or ordinary containment.

A circle may map to a line where it passes through the transformation’s pole.

### B.14.4 Jurisdictional Consequence

The tuple:

**(−1, 2, 2, 3)**

is chart-dependent.

Its numerical curvature values are not Möbius invariants.

The deeper inversive invariant is the tangency relation among generalized circles.

### B.14.5 Containment Roles

A circle functioning as an outer container in one Euclidean chart may appear as:

- an inner circle;
- a non-containing circle;
- or a line

in another chart.

Thus containerhood is not an intrinsic Euclidean species under Möbius transformation.

---

## B.15 Jordan Curve Theorem

### B.15.1 Exact Statement

A simple closed curve in the plane separates the plane into exactly two connected components:

- one bounded component;
- one unbounded component.

The curve is the boundary of both.

### B.15.2 Permitted Morphological Reading

The Spiral permits the disciplined translation:

> **Inside, outside, and boundary arise together through one differentiating contour.**

### B.15.3 Prohibited Overreach

The Jordan curve theorem does not prove:

- every living boundary is planar;
- every identity has one simple interior;
- or every relational distinction is binary.

---

## B.16 Möbius Strip and Local Orientability

### B.16.1 Exact Structure

The Möbius strip is a nonorientable surface with one boundary component.

Every sufficiently small neighborhood resembles an ordinary strip.

No globally consistent normal orientation can be extended around the whole surface.

### B.16.2 Permitted Morphological Reading

The Spiral permits:

> **Local two-sided distinction may remain genuine within a globally continuous nonorientable structure.**

### B.16.3 Prohibited Overreach

The Möbius strip does not prove:

- personal identity is literally nonorientable;
- all oppositions reverse under traversal;
- or every paradox signals hidden unity.

---

## B.17 Sphere Eversion

### B.17.1 Exact Statement

A two-dimensional sphere can be turned inside out in three-dimensional space through a regular homotopy of immersions.

The transformation:

- preserves the surface’s continuity;
- avoids tearing and creasing in the formal regular-homotopy sense;
- permits self-intersection.

### B.17.2 Permitted Morphological Reading

The result provides an exact exemplar of radical reorientation without severance.

### B.17.3 Prohibited Overreach

Sphere eversion does not prove:

- every transformation requires self-intersection;
- all self-contact is generative;
- or the universe is a sphere undergoing eversion.

---

## B.18 Klein Bottle

### B.18.1 Exact Structure

The abstract Klein bottle is a compact, boundaryless, nonorientable two-dimensional manifold.

It cannot be embedded in three-dimensional Euclidean space without self-intersection.

Standard three-dimensional images are immersions.

### B.18.2 Permitted Morphological Reading

The Spiral permits the question:

> **Is an apparent contradiction intrinsic to the structure, or produced by the dimensional limits of its rendering?**

### B.18.3 Prohibited Overreach

The Klein bottle does not establish that every contradiction becomes coherent in a higher-dimensional space.

---

## B.19 Knot Identity Under Ambient Isotopy

### B.19.1 Exact Setting

A knot is an embedding:

**K : S¹ → ℝ³**

considered up to ambient isotopy.

Two knots belong to the same knot type where one can be continuously deformed into the other through an ambient isotopy without cutting the embedded loop or passing it through itself.

### B.19.2 Diagrammatic Change

Different planar diagrams may represent the same knot.

Reidemeister moves preserve knot type.

Visible crossing count alone does not determine knot identity.

### B.19.3 Permitted Morphological Reading

The Spiral permits:

> **Identity may persist through substantial local deformation where the relevant global invariant remains preserved.**

### B.19.4 Prohibited Overreach

Knot theory does not prove that:

- a human self is literally a knot;
- psychological identity can be classified by knot polynomials;
- or every biographical crossing is a topological crossing.

---

## B.20 Fixed-Point and Incompleteness Neighbors

### B.20.1 Gödel

Gödel’s incompleteness theorems apply to consistent, effectively axiomatized formal systems sufficiently expressive to encode elementary arithmetic.

They do not apply automatically to every:

- mind;
- organism;
- institution;
- or self-referential process.

### B.20.2 Tarski

Tarski’s undefinability theorem concerns the impossibility, under stated conditions, of defining a full truth predicate for a sufficiently rich formal language entirely within that language.

It does not establish universal skepticism.

### B.20.3 Lawvere

Lawvere’s fixed-point theorem identifies categorical conditions under which certain morphisms imply fixed points for all endomorphisms of a specified object.

The result depends on the relevant categorical structure.

### B.20.4 Morphological Election

These formal neighbors bind formal systems assembled from enumerable statements, and — by morphological election — any local vantage's self-model:

> **No finite *local* rendering exhausts all conditions of its own rendering.**

The election is structurally informed by the exact neighbors, not a direct proof-theoretic corollary applied indiscriminately. Its scope is precise: it is a limit on the local vantage, not a claim that the whole has holes. A unitive whole is not a part-assembled formal system; it is understood from within by self-contact rather than self-enumeration, and its only genuine limit is that it cannot be verified from without, because there is no outside. The neighbors are therefore located, not universalized: they bound formal systems and local self-models, and the Spiral claims exactly those limits and no more.

---

**Descartes:**

**(k₁ + k₂ + k₃ + k₄)² = 2(k₁² + k₂² + k₃² + k₄²)**

**Fourth curvature:**

**k₄ = k₁ + k₂ + k₃ ± 2√(k₁k₂ + k₂k₃ + k₃k₁)**

**Completion radicand:**

**D = k₁k₂ + k₂k₃ + k₃k₁**

**Exact fit:**

**c = −n₁n₂ / (n₁ + n₂)**

**Radius form:**

**R = r₁ + r₂**

**Harmonic-mean relation:**

**|c| = H(n₁, n₂) / 2**

**Determinate fourth curvature:**

**x = n₁ + n₂ + c**

**x = (n₁² + n₁n₂ + n₂²) / (n₁ + n₂)**

**Reciprocal surpluses:**

**x − n₁ = n₂² / (n₁ + n₂)**

**x − n₂ = n₁² / (n₁ + n₂)**

**Product identity:**

**(x − n₁)(x − n₂) = c²**

**Geometric-mean form:**

**|c| = √((x − n₁)(x − n₂))**

**Equal-node case:**

**c = −n / 2**

**x = 3n / 2**

**Canonical normalization:**

**(−1, 2, 2, 3)**

**Descartes reflection:**

**k₄′ = 2(k₁ + k₂ + k₃) − k₄**

# Appendix C — Mathematical Jurisdiction Table

## C.1 Purpose

The jurisdiction table states:

- what each mathematical result proves;
- what morphological translation it permits;
- what it does not prove;
- and what language should be used when moving between registers.

---

## C.2 Core Jurisdiction Table

| Mathematical Object or Result | Exact Domain | What Is Proved | Permitted Morphological Translation | Not Proved |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| continuous self-map **f : X → X** | topology or algebra, depending on structure | a transformation begins and ends in one domain | a unitive system may transform internally | that every self-map is a generative fold |
| connectedness | topology | the space is not separated into disjoint nonempty open components | difference need not imply severance | that every relational system is topologically connected |
| fold icon | morphology informed by topology | no one universal theorem is claimed | local differentiation may arise through self-reconfiguration | a complete formal ontology of genesis |
| Jordan curve theorem | planar topology | a simple closed curve separates the plane into two components and is their common boundary | inside, outside, and boundary arise together | every identity is planar or has one simple boundary |
| Möbius strip | topology | local orientability can coexist with global nonorientability | local self/other distinction can coexist with deeper continuity | that persons are Möbius strips |
| sphere eversion | differential topology | a sphere can be regularly homotoped inside out through immersions | radical reorientation can occur without tearing | that all transformation requires self-intersection |
| Klein bottle | topology | the abstract surface cannot embed in ℝ³ without self-intersection | a rendering space may create apparent crossing | every contradiction is resolved by added dimension |
| knot identity | knot theory | knot type is preserved under ambient isotopy | identity can persist through lawful deformation | selves are literally knots |
| Descartes’ Circle Theorem | inversive geometry | four mutually tangent signed curvatures satisfy the quadratic relation | relational configuration can constrain completion | the Spiral proves relational metaphysics |
| completion radicand **D** | algebra of Descartes configurations | sign of **D** determines real algebraic completion regime | fit may distinguish incompatible, determinate, and plural completion | one scalar governs all living compatibility |
| exact-fit condition | inversive and Euclidean geometry | **c = −n₁n₂ / (n₁ + n₂)** and **R = r₁ + r₂** at repeated completion | a container may need to be fitted jointly to its nodes | larger or smaller rooms are morally better |
| harmonic-mean relation | arithmetic | **\|c\| = H(n₁,n₂)/2** | fit depends on both node curvatures | container curvature equals the harmonic mean |
| strict curvature increase | inversive geometry | **x > n₁** and **x > n₂** at exact fit | a fitted emergent may be more tightly organized than either node | the third is superior in worth or consciousness |
| reciprocal signature | inversive geometry | **(x−n₁)(x−n₂)=c²** | participants may bear different but related marks of one configuration | the container alone authors participant change |
| twin-gap reflection | Euclidean geometry in the canonical embedding | one curvature has two mirror-related placements | one invariant may admit plural incarnation | all truths have exactly two forms |
| Descartes reflection | Apollonian circle packing | alternate tangent curvature is **k₄′ = 2(k₁+k₂+k₃)−k₄** | a local update may preserve a lineage invariant | every recursion preserves meaning |
| integrality preservation | arithmetic of integer Descartes quadruples | integer curvature remains integer under reflection | lineage fidelity depends on local update rules | ethical fidelity follows automatically |
| Möbius transformation | inversive geometry | generalized circles, angles, incidence, and tangency are preserved | roles such as container may be chart-relative | Euclidean curvature and containment are invariant |
| Yoneda lemma | category theory | an object is determined up to isomorphism by its represented relational behavior | relation-first descriptions can recover object identity | all ontology is nothing but relation |
| Lawvere fixed-point theorem | category theory | stated representability conditions imply fixed points | sufficiently expressive self-reference has formal structural consequences | every self-referential system satisfies the hypotheses |
| Gödel incompleteness | mathematical logic | specified formal systems cannot prove every arithmetical truth expressible within them | internal closure may have limits | minds or institutions are automatically Gödel-incomplete |
| Tarski undefinability | mathematical logic | truth for a sufficiently rich language cannot be fully defined inside that language under the Spiral’s assumptions | local renderings may require a wider metalanguage | truth is impossible |
| attractor and basin | dynamical systems | formally defined trajectories converge under specified dynamics | habits and recognition may be modeled through basin structure | every psychological state is literally one attractor |
| bifurcation | dynamical systems | parameter change produces qualitative change in system dynamics | recognition may alter the operative phase portrait | underlying state-space topology must change |
| hysteresis | dynamical systems | transition threshold depends on direction and history | nucleation and maintenance may require different conditions | every relational persistence exhibits one hysteresis law |
| standing wave | wave physics | a spatially structured oscillatory mode persists under boundary and phase conditions | some thirds persist as patterned activity | every third is literally a wave |
| coherent emission | optics or field physics | phase-related emission can produce stable interference and directed output | radiance requires recoverable phase | ethical radiance is proved by laser physics |
